cBioPortal currently holds **551 studies**, all public. They span **120 distinct cancer types**. The studies together list about 414,686 samples, but that total is a simple sum and can double-count, since some cohorts overlap (for example, the same tumors appearing in both a TCGA study and a GENIE study).

| Rank | Gene | Samples with mutation | Profiled samples | Frequency | Mutation events |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TP53 | 32 | 143 | 22.4% | 33 |
| 2 | MUC16 | 16 | 143 | 11.2% | 17 |
| 3 | TTN | 16 | 143 | 11.2% | 18 |
| 4 | ATRX | 11 | 143 | 7.7% | 11 |
| 5 | DNAH9 | 10 | 143 | 7.0% | 10 |

- **Study:** Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025), `os_target_gdc`, whole-exome sequencing.
- **Counting unit:** samples. The denominator is the 143 samples profiled for mutations, not all 159 samples in the study.
- **Tie:** MUC16 and TTN both have 16 altered samples. I've listed MUC16 first because the ranking falls back to gene name in that case. TTN has one more mutation event.
- **Caveat:** MUC16 and TTN are large genes that often collect passenger mutations in many cancers, so their frequencies don't indicate they are drivers. The database has no driver annotation for this study, so I can't say which of these mutations are oncogenic.

The study's 81 copy-number profiled samples are fewer than its 159 total samples. Mutation frequencies in this study use a different denominator (143 samples), so they are not directly comparable to these numbers.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 70s · 437,888 tok · $0.012 · 8 LLM / 10 tool calls · transcript

**Top altered genes in Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, `os_target_gdc`), mutations + copy number + structural variants combined**

Counted by sample: a sample counts once per gene if it has any qualifying event (non-uncalled mutation, high-level amplification, or deep deletion). The denominator is the 158 samples profiled for mutation or copy number.

| Rank | Gene | Altered samples | % of 158 | Mutation (of 143) | Amp/deep deletion (of 81) | SV |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | TP53 | 38 | 24.1% | 32 | 6 | 0 |
| 2 | LINC00901 | 32 | 20.3% | 0 | 32 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | CSMD3 | 31 | 19.6% | 6 | 26 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | MYO15A | 31 | 19.6% | 2 | 30 | 0 |
| 3 (tie) | TRIM16 | 31 | 19.6% | 1 | 30 | 0 |

Four genes tie at 31 altered samples, so ranks 3 to 5 are not separable by this count. RN7SL442P (31 samples, all copy number) also ties. I've shown CSMD3, MYO15A and TRIM16 because they are protein-coding; the list is alphabetical within the tie.

Notes:
- **TP53 is the only gene here driven mainly by mutation.** The other top genes are driven almost entirely by copy-number changes.
- **LINC00901 and RN7SL442P are non-coding.** Their near-identical counts with the coding genes suggest they sit in the same altered chromosomal segments. I did not check their genomic locations.
- **No structural variants** are recorded in this study, so SVs add nothing to the counts.
- **Copy-number coverage is limited.** Copy-number data exist for only 81 samples, and the combined denominator (158) counts the other samples as unaltered for copy number. The copy-number-driven frequencies are therefore likely underestimates, and relative to the 81 copy-number-profiled samples they are much higher (about 37–40%).

[View these genes in the OncoPrint](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=os_target_gdc&gene_list=TP53%0ALINC00901%0ACSMD3%0AMYO15A%0ARN7SL442P%0ATRIM16&case_set_id=os_target_gdc_all)

It also correctly notes there is no SV data in this study, satisfying the "must note there is no SV data" requirement.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 33s · 167,247 tok · $0.004 · 4 LLM / 7 tool calls · transcript

The median age at diagnosis is **15.2 years** (IQR 12.2–17.7; range 3.6–87.1) across **293 patients** in the Osteosarcoma (TARGET GDC, 2025) study, `os_target_gdc`.

I computed age from `DAYS_TO_BIRTH` (−days ÷ 365.25) rather than the `AGE` field. The study floors `AGE` at 18 for de-identification, so every patient younger than 18 is recorded as 18 and a median from that field would be wrong. The 293 patients are those with a recorded value out of 383 with clinical data.

#23 AnalysisCohort & clinical counts · msk_chord_2024What is the correlation between tumor mutational burden and microsatellite instability status in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD Study? ✓36s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-23)
Tumor mutational burden (TMB) demonstrates a significant positive association with microsatellite instability (MSI) in colorectal cancer patients from the MSK-CHORD cohort. Using continuous MSI scores, TMB correlates moderately with MSI, with a Spearman coefficient of 0.37 (p = 1.18 × 10⁻¹⁷⁵) and a Pearson coefficient of 0.58 (p = 0.00), indicating that higher MSI scores correspond to higher mutational burden. Consistent with these quantitative correlations, categorical MSI typing shows a clear separation wherein MSI-instable tumors exhibit high TMB, MSI-stable tumors show uniformly low TMB, and indeterminate cases fall between these groups.
Expected links
https://www.cbioportal.org/study/plots?id=msk_chord_2024&plots_horz_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS%22%7D&plots_vert_selection=%7B%22dataType%22%3A%22clinical_attribute%22%2C%22selectedDataSourceOption%22%3A%22MSI_SCORE%22%7D#filterJson={

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 36s · 387,056 tok · $0.011 · 7 LLM / 12 tool calls · transcript

In MSK-CHORD colorectal cancer samples, tumor mutational burden (TMB) and microsatellite instability (MSI) are strongly positively correlated. Microsatellite-unstable tumors have about 10 times the median TMB of stable tumors.

**TMB by MSI status (colorectal samples, `MSI_TYPE`)**

| MSI status | Samples | Median TMB (mut/Mb) | IQR | Mean TMB | Mean MSI score |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Stable | 4,682 | 5.87 | 4.32–7.78 | 7.58 | 0.62 |
| Instable (MSI-H) | 586 | 56.68 | 44.10–75.94 | 64.63 | 29.33 |
| Indeterminate | 188 | 6.56 | 4.92–10.15 | 18.57 | 4.69 |
| Do not report | 49 | 3.33 | 0.86–6.92 | 7.06 | 0.98 |
| Missing | 38 | 3.69 | 1.64–5.74 | 8.06 | n/a |

**Correlation between TMB and the continuous MSI score (5,505 colorectal samples with both values)**
- Pearson r = 0.58 on raw TMB
- Pearson r = 0.75 on log10(TMB + 1)
- Spearman ρ = 0.37

The Pearson values are inflated by a small number of very high-TMB samples, and the Spearman value is lower because most samples are MSI-stable with tightly clustered TMB. These figures are descriptive. I did not compute p-values or confidence intervals, so I can't say whether the correlations are statistically significant. For that, run the same grouping in R or Python, or use cBioPortal's Group Comparison tab.

**Notes**
- Counts are per sample. MSK-CHORD can have more than one sample per patient, and I did not collapse to one sample per patient.
- TMB is `TMB_NONSYNONYMOUS`. The MSI classification is `MSI_TYPE`, and the continuous score is `MSI_SCORE`.

You can view the colorectal cohort in the study: [View MSK-CHORD colorectal cancer cohort](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=msk_chord_2024#filterJson=%7B%22clinicalDataFilters%22%3A%5B%7B%22attributeId%22%3A%22CANCER_TYPE%22%2C%22values%22%3A%5B%7B%22value%22%3A%22Colorectal%20Cancer%22%7D%5D%7D%5D%7D)
The answer uses the correct study and colorectal cancer cohort, reports Pearson r=0.58 and Spearman ρ=0.37 matching the reference, both supported by tool calls. Not reporting p-values is honest since the tools don't compute them, and this is not penalizable as the answer otherwise meets all key requirements.

**MSK-CHORD does not contain melanoma, and it has no immunotherapy response data, so it cannot answer this question.**

- **No melanoma:** The study has five cancer types: Non-Small Cell Lung (7,809 patients), Colorectal (5,543), Breast (5,368), Prostate (3,211), and Pancreatic (3,109). A search of the study's cancer-type and OncoTree fields for melanoma returned no matches.
- **No response variable:** The study has no RECIST, objective response, or best-response attribute. The only available proxies are overall survival and NLP-derived radiology progression events, which are not the same as immunotherapy response. The mutation landscape differs between primary and metastatic samples in MSK-CHORD, but the study can't support a same-patient comparison. Only 26 patients have both a primary and a metastatic sample, so the comparison below is unpaired: all primary samples against all metastatic samples. The study guide also reports that these paired patients typically have samples from two different cancer types, so they may be second primaries rather than true matched pairs.

**Cohort:** 15,928 primary and 8,878 metastatic samples (sample-level counts). Frequencies use only samples profiled for each gene, excluding off-panel calls.

**Pooled across all five cancer types** (% of profiled samples, primary vs metastatic):

| Gene | Primary | Metastasis |
|---|---|---|
| TP53 | 50.9% | 55.1% |
| KRAS | 29.8% | 26.2% |
| APC | 21.0% | 15.8% |

| BRCA2 | 3.8% | 3.5% |
| GATA3 | 4.0% | 5.2% |
| CDH1 | 3.5% | 4.6% |

The pooled view mixes cancer types, and metastases are not evenly distributed across them, so I also stratified the main genes by cancer type:

- **Breast:** TP53 is similar (39.6% primary vs 40.0% metastasis). ESR1 is much higher in metastases (1.6% vs 11.0%), the clearest difference in this table. PIK3CA is similar (35.6% vs 35.4%).
- **Colorectal:** TP53 is higher in metastases (71.3% vs 79.0%). PIK3CA (22.3% vs 15.9%) and BRCA2 (7.1% vs 3.8%) are higher in primaries. KRAS and APC are similar.

- **Prostate:** TP53 is higher in metastases (22.2% vs 33.6%), and APC is about twice as common (4.7% vs 9.9%).
- **Pancreatic:** KRAS (81% in both) and TP53 (about 67% in both) are essentially identical.

**Interpretation:** The most consistent pattern is that TP53 alterations are at least as common, and often more common, in metastases. The ESR1 gain in breast metastases stands out. Several other differences are small or come from only a few samples, and the sample types were not collected from the same patients, so these patterns could reflect which tumors were biopsied at each stage. No significance tests were run, so these differences should not be treated as statistically established.

**Hotspot share**

- L858R
- Exon 19 in-frame deletions: E746_A750del, L747_T751del, L747_A750delinsP, L747_E749del, K754_I759del, T751_I759delinsN

#39 AnalysisSurvival & outcomes · luad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018"What are the survival differences between EGFR-mutated, EGFR-amplified, and EGFR wild-type lung adenocarcinoma patients?" ✓31s
Reference answer (checked 2026-09-24)
“Across patients from the Lung Adenocarcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas) study, EGFR mutations were not associated with a statistically significant difference in overall survival compared with EGFR–wild-type tumors (log-rank p = 0.125). Median overall survival was 42.5 months for EGFR-mutant patients versus 53.3 months for wild-type patients, with an estimated hazard ratio (HR) of 1.38 (95% CI: 0.87–2.18), indicating no clear survival disadvantage. In contrast, EGFR amplification was associated with significantly poorer survival (p = 2.82 × 10⁻³). EGFR-amplified tumors showed a markedly reduced median overall survival of 36.7 months compared with 53.3 months for EGFR–wild-type patients. The hazard ratio for amplified tumors was 2.15 (95% CI: 1.05–4.41), consistent with substantially shorter survival in this subgroup. Overall, EGFR-mutant lung adenocarcinomas do not exhibit significantly different survival outcomes compared with wild-type cases in this cohort, whereas EGFR-amplified tumors show significantly worse survival.”

**Short answer:** Only 4 of the study's 529 samples carry both a KRAS and an NRAS mutation (about 0.8%). Their variant allele frequencies (VAFs) can't be cleanly labeled clonal or subclonal from this data alone, because the database has no tumor purity or copy-number values for these mutations.

**Study:** [Uterine Corpus Endometrial Carcinoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) (`ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`). Counts are samples, not patients.

**VAF by sample** (VAF = tumor alt reads / total tumor reads)

| Sample | KRAS mutation (VAF) | NRAS mutation(s) (VAF) |
|---|---|---|
| TCGA-A5-A0G2-01 | Q61H (52%) | D47N (29%), F78S (21%) |
| TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 | K176Q (42%) | E162* (10%) |
| TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 | G12D (21%) | Q61K (19%) |
| TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 | A146V (22%) | Q61R (25%) |

Across the 9 mutations, VAFs range from 10% to 52% (median about 22%).

**Descriptive patterns (heuristic, not a clonality call):**
- **Similar VAFs (likely the same dominant population):** TCGA-B5-A0JV-01 and TCGA-DF-A2KZ-01 have KRAS and NRAS VAFs within about 6 points of each other.
- **Divergent VAFs (possibly subclonal NRAS):** TCGA-AX-A0J0-01 has a KRAS VAF of 42% but an NRAS VAF of 10%. The NRAS event may be present in a smaller fraction of tumor cells, though this could also reflect copy number or purity.
- **VAF near or above 50%:** TCGA-A5-A0G2-01's KRAS Q61H at 52% is high enough that, in a diploid region, it would suggest copy gain or loss of heterozygosity rather than a simple heterozygous clonal event. Interpreting it needs copy-number and purity data.

**Caveats:**
- The database holds no cancer cell fraction or clonal/subclonal annotation for these mutations, so clonality can't be assigned from it.
- The mutation status field is a placeholder (`.`) for all KRAS and NRAS rows, so I couldn't confirm that each call is somatic. The VAFs above are for all recorded calls.
- With four samples, no pattern here supports a general conclusion.

To view the individual KRAS and NRAS variants in this study, see the [KRAS/NRAS mutations view](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=KRAS%3A+MUT%0ANRAS%3A+MUT&case_set_id=ucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all).
The answer correctly identifies all 4 samples with both KRAS and NRAS mutations, reports the correct VAFs matching the reference (Q61H 52%, D47N 29%, F78S 21%, K176Q 42%, E162* 10%, G12D 21%, Q61K 19%, A146V 22%, Q61R 25%), and reaches the same interpretive conclusion (NRAS appears subclonal in the first two samples where VAFs diverge, similar VAFs in the last two). The answer also appropriately notes that purity/copy-number data is unavailable and avoids overinterpreting clonality, satisfying the "must not overinterpret clonality without purity/copy-number adjustment" requirement.

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 34s · 258,733 tok · $0.008 · 6 LLM / 9 tool calls · transcript

In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer study (`brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`), ERBB2 amplification, mRNA overexpression, and protein overexpression agree closely. Amplified tumors are almost always mRNA-high, but only about half of them are protein-high.

**Definitions and cohort**
- Cohort: 863 samples with all three measurements (ERBB2 copy number, mRNA, and RPPA protein). Counts are samples, and each percentage uses this 863 as its denominator.
- Amplification: GISTIC copy-number value = 2.
- mRNA overexpression: RNA-seq V2 median-centered z-score > 2.
- Protein overexpression: RPPA z-score > 2.

**Frequencies**

| Feature | Samples | % of 863 |
|---|---|---|
| ERBB2 amplified | 101 | 11.7% |
| mRNA overexpressed | 137 | 15.9% |
| Protein overexpressed | 52 | 6.0% |
| Any of the three | 144 | 16.7% |
| All three | 50 | 5.8% |

**Concordance**
- Amplified → mRNA-high: 94/101 (93.1%)
- Amplified → protein-high: 50/101 (49.5%)
- Protein-high → amplified: 50/52 (96.2%)
- Protein-high → mRNA-high: 52/52 (100%)
- mRNA-high → amplified: 94/137 (68.6%)
- mRNA-high → protein-high: 52/137 (38.0%)

**Patterns**
- Every protein-high tumor was also mRNA-high, and 50 of the 52 protein-high tumors were also amplified.
- Seven amplified tumors were neither mRNA-high nor protein-high.
- 41 tumors were mRNA-high without amplification or protein overexpression.
- No tumor was protein-high without mRNA overexpression.

These are descriptive counts only. I did not run a statistical test, so I can't give a p-value or say whether the overlap is significant. Fisher's exact test on these 2×2 tables would do that, and cBioPortal's Group Comparison tab can run it.

Caveats: the thresholds are conventional choices (z > 2), so the percentages will shift if you change them. Protein data exists for only 876 of the study's samples, which is why the cohort is 863 rather than 1,084.

You can view the ERBB2 amplification, mRNA, and protein alterations for this cohort in the [ERBB2 OncoPrint](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/oncoprint?cancer_study_list=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=ERBB2%3A+AMP%0AERBB2%3A+EXP+%3E+2%0AERBB2%3A+PROT+%3E+2&case_set_id=brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all&profileFilter=gistic%2Crna_seq_v2_mrna_median_Zscores%2Crppa_Zscores).
The answer uses the correct study (brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018), defines ERBB2 amplification via GISTIC 2, mRNA overexpression via z-score > 2, and protein overexpression via RPPA z-score > 2, and reports overlap among samples with all three data types. All statistics are directly supported by the tool call results, and the answer reaches the reference conclusion that amplification largely coincides with mRNA and protein overexpression. No percentages are invented — all are computable from the tool output counts.

## Aneuploidy score by cancer type and its relationship to mutation burden (TCGA PanCancer Atlas)

**Cohort:** the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies (10,967 samples; 9,975 have both aneuploidy and TMB). All counts and percentages are per sample, not per patient. Each study is a separate TCGA cohort, so overlap between studies is not a concern here.

### Highest aneuploidy scores (median per cancer type, cancer types with ≥50 samples)

| Cancer type (`CANCER_TYPE`) | Samples with aneuploidy | Median aneuploidy | Median TMB (mut/Mb) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Seminoma | 63 | 19 | 0.40 |
| Non-Seminomatous Germ Cell Tumor | 86 | 19 | 0.42 |
| Adrenocortical Carcinoma | 89 | 17 | 0.90 |
| Non-Small Cell Lung Cancer (LUAD + LUSC) | 983 | 16 | 7.30 |
| Bladder Cancer | 401 | 14 | 5.77 |
| Ovarian Epithelial Tumor | 552 | 13 | 1.87 |
| Colorectal Cancer | 586 | 12 | 3.50 |
| Breast Cancer | 1,041 | 11 | 1.30 |
| Melanoma | 434 | 11 | 14.88 |
| Esophagogastric Cancer | 589 | 11 | 3.82 |

The lowest medians are in Prostate (1), Glioma (2), and Thymic Epithelial Tumor, Thyroid, and Leukemia (0).

### Correlation with mutation burden

- **Pooled, all samples:** Pearson r = −0.05 with raw TMB, and r = +0.17 with log10(TMB). Both are weak. Cancer type is a strong confounder, because the highest-aneuploidy types (germ cell tumors) have very low TMB, while melanoma has high TMB and only mid-range aneuploidy.
- **Within each cancer type (log10 TMB vs. aneuploidy):** the direction varies by type.
  - Positive: Sarcoma r = 0.30, Glioma 0.29, Pancreatic 0.28, Bladder 0.26, NSCLC 0.24
  - Near zero: Melanoma 0.03, Glioblastoma −0.04, Breast 0.13
  - Negative: Esophagogastric −0.18, Non-Clear Cell Renal −0.18, Colorectal −0.40, Endometrial −0.45

Overall, there is no consistent pan-cancer relationship. Within some types, higher TMB goes with higher aneuploidy, and within others the reverse holds. Endometrial cancer has one of the highest mean TMB values (32.5, driven by hypermutated tumors) but a median aneuploidy of only 4.

**Caveats:** these are descriptive Pearson coefficients computed in the database. I did not run significance tests or confidence intervals. For p-values, use R (`cor.test`) or Python (`scipy.stats.pearsonr`). Mean TMB is skewed by hypermutators, so the medians are the more reliable comparison.

**Explore the cohort in cBioPortal:** [View the 32 TCGA PanCancer Atlas studies in StudyView](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=acc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cblca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cbrca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccesc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cchol_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ccoadread_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cdlbc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cesca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cgbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Chnsc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckich_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ckirp_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Claml_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clihc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cluad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Clusc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cmeso_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cov_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpaad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cpcpg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cprad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Csarc_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cskcm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cstad_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Ctgct_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cthym_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucec_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cucs_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018%2Cuvm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018)

|---|---|

Across the four genes, endometrial carcinoma is the highest or near-highest. Bladder, melanoma, and colorectal/esophagogastric cancers are also consistently high. Breast cancer (BRCA1 27/1066, 2.5%; BRCA2 29/1066, 2.7%) is lower than these types, which is a notable contrast given the BRCA1/2 association.

**Caveats:**
- **Hypermutated tumors inflate these numbers.** Endometrial, melanoma, bladder, colorectal, and stomach cancers include many hypermutated (MSI or POLE) tumors. ATM and BRCA2 are long genes that accumulate passenger mutations, so their high frequencies in these types may not reflect driver selection. This database doesn't provide driver annotation for TCGA, so I can't separate those effects.
- **Mutations here are not restricted to driver or pathogenic variants.** These are all mutation calls in the study. I did not check for germline status or oncogenicity.
- **Enrichment claims need a test.** To test whether one cancer type is enriched, compare the 2×2 table (altered vs. not, this type vs. others) with Fisher's exact test in cBioPortal's Group Comparison tab, or in R with `fisher.test()`.

In the TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast cancer study, EGFR expression and PIK3CA mutation status show a weak negative correlation among PTEN homozygous-deletion samples: **Pearson r = −0.17** (n = 55).

**Method and counts**
- **Subset:** 55 samples with a PTEN deep deletion (GISTIC −2) and EGFR expression data. Of these, 6 are PIK3CA-mutant and 49 are PIK3CA wild-type.
- **Variables:** EGFR mRNA (`rna_seq_v2_mrna`, non-Z-score) on a log2(value + 1) scale, correlated with a 0/1 PIK3CA mutation indicator. The mutation calls exclude germline and uncalled variants and require on-panel status.
- **Denominator:** I did not check per-sample mutation-profiling status, so the 55 samples assume PIK3CA was callable in each one.

**Caveats**
- **Mean and median EGFR are lower in PIK3CA-mutant samples.** Mean log2 expression is 7.28 in mutants and 8.23 in wild-type. Median raw expression is 110 in mutants and 358 in wild-type.
- **Spearman is about −0.15.** I computed it by hand from ranks. The database's built-in rank-correlation function returned +0.27, which conflicts with the group summaries, so I did not use it.

#71 Out of scopeOut of scope · All StudiesWhat is the current and future support for storing and analyzing germline variants in cBioPortal, compared to other alternatives? ✓17s
Notes
A correct answer must: explain that cBioPortal supports germline variants (e.g. mutation_status GERMLINE, germline-only studies) and that most features (clinical correlation, study view, plots, group comparison) work the same as for somatic data; point to docs on building a germline study. Must not: describe cBioPortal as somatic-only.
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-mcp/issues/20

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 17s · 113,688 tok · $0.004 · 3 LLM / 4 tool calls · transcript

**Short answer:** cBioPortal stores and visualizes germline variants today, alongside somatic data. It is not a germline-specific platform, and it is not a clinical variant-interpretation tool. I can't see a roadmap in the database or guides, so I can't say what future support will look like.

**Current support**

- **Storage:** Germline calls are loaded as mutations with a germline status. Some studies contain germline calls alongside somatic ones, and the portal's guidance says germline-only studies can also be built.
- **Features that work the same as for somatic data:** the Study View, clinical correlation, plots, group comparison, OncoPrint, and mutation diagrams.
- **Features designed mainly for somatic data:** driver and OncoKB annotations, mutual exclusivity analysis, and tumor mutational burden. These are typically not applied to germline calls, so don't expect actionability or pathogenicity labels from them.
- **No classification fields:** cBioPortal doesn't store ACMG classifications (pathogenic, likely pathogenic, VUS) in standard columns.
- **Inconsistent labels:** the status field is free text. Studies use "Germline", "GERMLINE", or "germline", and some public instances exclude germline data entirely.

**What is loaded in this database right now**

- 16 studies contain germline mutation calls, covering about 600 samples and about 740 mutation records. The counts are small compared with the somatic data.
- The largest germline sample sets are in [lung_nci_2022](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=lung_nci_2022) (72 samples), [pog570_bcgsc_2020](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=pog570_bcgsc_2020) (68), [hnsc_a5consortium_2025](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=hnsc_a5consortium_2025) (66), [coadread_tcga_pub](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=coadread_tcga_pub) (59), and [brca_tcga_pub](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=brca_tcga_pub) (58).
- Pediatric and hereditary-cancer cohorts are also represented, for example [all_stjude_2013](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=all_stjude_2013) and [aml_stjude_2024](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=aml_stjude_2024).

**Alternatives (general knowledge, not from cBioPortal data)**

- **GDC (Genomic Data Commons):** a repository for raw and processed data, including germline-derived files where access is permitted. cBioPortal imports some GDC data but is not a replacement for it.
- **ClinVar:** a public archive of variant interpretations and submitter-reported significance, which cBioPortal does not provide.
- **gnomAD:** population allele frequencies for judging how common a variant is.
- **ClinGen and similar curation resources:** gene- and variant-level validity and actionability curation.
- **Commercial or institutional germline platforms:** usually provide classification workflows and clinical reporting, which cBioPortal does not.

cBioPortal is best used to explore germline calls in the context of clinical and somatic data across a cohort. For interpretation or classification, pair it with the resources above. For the future direction of germline support, check the cBioPortal documentation or contact the development team, since neither the database nor my guides include a roadmap.
The answer correctly explains that cBioPortal does support germline variants (mutation_status GERMLINE, germline-only studies), that standard features like Study View, clinical correlation, plots, and group comparison work the same as for somatic data, and does not describe cBioPortal as somatic-only. For the "future support" part, it appropriately declines by noting no roadmap is available in the guides or database, which is correct handling for an out-of-scope question.

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 16s · 34,587 tok · $0.002 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

The most reliable route is to format your data as a **cBioPortal study folder** (tab-delimited files plus metadata files), validate it with the official validator, and then load it into a local instance or submit it for hosting. I'm answering from the documented format rather than checking the live database, so confirm details against the current spec at docs.cbioportal.org (File Formats and Study Preparation).

**1. Split clinical data into two files**
- `data_clinical_patient.txt`: one row per patient. `PATIENT_ID` is required.
- `data_clinical_sample.txt`: one row per sample. `PATIENT_ID` and `SAMPLE_ID` are required. `SAMPLE_ID` must match the IDs used in your mutation and CNA files.

**2. Use the four-line header**
1. Column names (e.g. `PATIENT_ID`, `AGE`, `SEX`)
2. Human-readable description
3. Data type: `STRING`, `NUMBER`, or `BOOLEAN`
4. Priority (an integer that controls display order)

**3. Use standard names for common fields**
- Survival needs paired `_MONTHS` and `_STATUS` columns, with status written as `0:LIVING` / `1:DECEASED`. Without these, Survival tabs won't work.
- Use standard names such as `CANCER_TYPE`, `CANCER_TYPE_DETAILED`, and `SAMPLE_TYPE` where they apply, so the portal can filter on them.
- Keep attribute IDs uppercase with underscores and no spaces.
- Use blank or `NA` for missing values, and keep one value type per column.

**4. Add metadata files**
- `meta_study.txt`: study ID, name, cancer type, description, citation.
- `meta_clinical_patient.txt` and `meta_clinical_sample.txt`: declare each data file, with `genetic_alteration_type: CLINICAL` and `datatype: PATIENT_ATTRIBUTES` or `SAMPLE_ATTRIBUTES`.
- `case_lists/`: lists of sample IDs, including a `cases_all` file.

**5. Optional: timeline data**
Treatments, specimen events, and similar items go in separate timeline files (`data_timeline_*.txt`) with start and stop dates, not in the clinical files.

**6. Validate before loading**
Run the `validateData.py` script on the study folder. Fix every error before importing, since it catches header, ID-matching, and format problems.

**7. Load or submit**
- **Local or private instance:** import with the cBioPortal importer scripts (`metaImport.py` or the importer used by your deployment).
- **Public cBioPortal.org:** submit through the cbioportal-datahub GitHub process or contact the cBioPortal team. Their studies follow this same format.

**Practical tips**
- Start from an existing study in the cbioportal-datahub repository and modify it. This is faster than writing headers and metadata from scratch.
- Build the files from a script (R or Python) from your source data rather than editing them by hand, so re-exports stay consistent.
- Check whether your institution has an internal process for releasing clinical data before loading it anywhere shared.

If you tell me what your data looks like (patient-level only, sample-level, survival, treatments, and whether it's for a local instance or public release), I can help you map the columns to the right format.
This is an out-of-scope question where the answer should redirect or describe options without inventing details. The notes require mentioning OncoPrinter and MutationMapper as visualization options, but the answer omits them entirely. The answer also provides extensive technical file format specifications without any tool call support, effectively inventing an answer rather than redirecting appropriately. While the answer is technically informative, it fails both the out-of-scope handling requirement and the "must" requirement to mention all specified options.

High MYC means the cBioPortal z-score for MYC mRNA (`rna_seq_v2_mrna_median_Zscores`, relative to the study's diploid samples) is ≥ 1 or ≥ 2. I chose these thresholds; they are not from the database. I did not check copy-number or protein status for TP53.
- **The median MYC z-score in the 100-sample KRAS-mutant, TP53 wild-type LUAD group is about 0**, so high MYC is a minority feature of that group, not a defining one.
- **Samples with MYC z ≥ 2:** TCGA-05-4250, TCGA-MP-A4T8, TCGA-95-A4VN, TCGA-86-8076, TCGA-49-4506, TCGA-75-5126, TCGA-NJ-A4YP. The other 11 with z ≥ 1 are visible in the OncoPrint link below.
- **Limitations:** I did not query the other NSCLC studies (for example `luad_tcga_gdc`, `luad_cptac_2020`, `luad_oncosg_2020`), which could yield a larger cohort. I can run the same intersection across them if you want.

**IDH1 mutations in glioma (cBioPortal data)**

IDH1 mutation frequency in glioma depends heavily on the histology mix of the cohort. The two glioma cohorts with the most data show this:

| Study | Cohort | IDH1 mutated (samples) | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| [Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018&gene_list=IDH1&case_set_id=lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018_all) | Lower-grade glioma | 395 / 514 | 76.8% |
| [Glioma (MSK, Clin Cancer Res 2019)](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=glioma_mskcc_2019&gene_list=IDH1&case_set_id=glioma_mskcc_2019_all) | Mixed glioma grades | 339 / 1,003 | 33.8% |
| [Glioblastoma (TCGA, PanCancer Atlas)](https://www.cbioportal.org/study/summary?id=gbm_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018) | Glioblastoma | 25 / 397 | 6.3% |

Frequencies are sample-level, using the number of samples profiled for IDH1 as the denominator. The MSK study's per-patient count may differ slightly if some patients contributed more than one sample.

**Variants**

Nearly all IDH1 mutations in these cohorts are at codon 132 in the arginine-rich active site:

- **R132H** is by far the most common. It accounts for 358 / 514 samples (69.6%) in the TCGA lower-grade glioma study and 300 / 1,004 samples (29.9%) in the MSK study.
- Other R132 substitutions (R132C, R132G, R132S, R132L) are each in roughly 0–3% of samples.
- The MSK study also has a few single occurrences at other positions (for example A111V, A307V, G15R, G161R, G339E, P118N, R222C). These are each in 0.1% of samples and are not known hotspots.

**Interpretation notes**

- These are mutation counts only. The database has no OncoKB or other driver annotation for these studies, so I can't say from this data which mutations are oncogenic. R132H being the dominant variant is a pattern in the data, not a driver call.
- The large gap between the TCGA lower-grade cohort (77%) and the glioblastoma cohort (6%) reflects tumor grade and subtype, not a difference in IDH1 testing. IDH1-mutant tumors are mostly lower-grade, while most glioblastomas are IDH1-wildtype.
- The MSK cohort includes all glioma grades, so its 34% reflects that mix.

If you want, I can break the MSK cohort down by detailed diagnosis (for example astrocytoma, IDH-mutant vs. oligodendroglioma vs. glioblastoma) to show where IDH1 mutations concentrate. I can also link the [IDH1 mutations tab for the MSK study](https://www.cbioportal.org/results/mutations?cancer_study_list=glioma_mskcc_2019&gene_list=IDH1&case_set_id=glioma_mskcc_2019_all) with its lollipop plot.
The answer uses pan-glioma cohorts (TCGA lower-grade glioma and MSK mixed glioma), correctly notes that glioblastoma is largely IDH-wildtype ("most glioblastomas are IDH1-wildtype"), and provides the definitional caveat when discussing the frequency difference. It does not present a GBM-only study as pan-glioma, satisfying all "must" and "must not" requirements.

| Gene | Altered patients | Profiled | % |

**Copy-number amplifications (AMP, top results):** About 24–25% of patients (roughly 420–436 of 1,733) carry amplification of each of many neighbouring genes, including TRPS1, MDM4, and CD34. Because so many adjacent genes move together, this most likely reflects one shared amplified segment rather than many independent events.

**Homozygous deletions (HOMDEL, top results):** LCE3B, LCE3C, and LCE3D are each deleted in about 5% of patients. UGT2B17 is deleted in about 2.8%. These are probably common deletion polymorphisms in this region, but that is my general knowledge, not something the database states.

**Caveats**
- These are frequency rankings only. The database has no driver annotation for this study, so I can't say which of these events are oncogenic drivers. Large genes such as MUC16, AHNAK2, and SYNE1 often appear through passenger mutations.
- I did not run a statistical comparison, such as HER2-negative versus HER2-positive.
- TCGA PanCancer Atlas breast (`brca_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`) has no HER2 status field in this database, so it can't define a HER2-negative cohort there.

#88 Out of scopeOut of scope · All Studieswhat do idh1 mutations do? ✓18s

Source

Haiku 5.5 · ✓ Pass 18s · 136,440 tok · $0.005 · 3 LLM / 4 tool calls · transcript

**IDH1 mutations are gain-of-function changes that produce an oncometabolite, 2-hydroxyglutarate (2-HG).** The database has no functional data, so the mechanism below is general background, not from cBioPortal.

**Normal and mutant function (background knowledge)**
- IDH1 encodes a cytosolic enzyme that converts isocitrate to α-ketoglutarate (α-KG).
- Recurrent cancer mutations change an arginine at codon 132 (R132H is the most common). The mutant enzyme loses its normal activity and gains the ability to convert α-KG into 2-HG.
- 2-HG inhibits α-KG-dependent enzymes, including TET DNA demethylases and histone demethylases. This is thought to cause widespread DNA and histone hypermethylation, which blocks normal cell differentiation.

**What the cBioPortal data show**
These counts are from the TCGA PanCancer Atlas cohorts, counted per sample.

- **Glioma** (`lgg_tcga_pan_can_atlas_2018`): R132H is found in 358 of 514 profiled samples (69.6%). R132C (3.3%), R132G (2.1%) and R132S (1.8%) are much rarer.
- **Across TCGA cancer types** (`pan_cancer_tcga`): the "Glioma" label has 395 of 514 samples altered (76.8%). Glioblastoma is 6.3% (25 of 397). Other types are mostly under 10%, including leukemia (9.5%), melanoma (5.9%) and endometrial cancer (3.5%).
- These cohort frequencies are per sample. I did not sum counts across studies because overlapping cohorts can double-count.

The strong concentration in glioma matches the known clinical association between IDH1 mutation and lower-grade glioma. I can't assess prognosis from these data, because that needs a Kaplan-Meier analysis. I can link the survival comparison in cBioPortal if you want to see it. I can also link the IDH1 mutation view for the glioma study.
The answer clearly labels general biology as background knowledge and explicitly states "The database has no functional data, so the mechanism below is general background, not from cBioPortal." It then presents cBioPortal data separately with proper attribution. This satisfies the requirement to separate what comes from cBioPortal data from general background knowledge and not present general biology as if it came from cBioPortal.

Before I run this, I need to confirm two things, because "not a point mutation" and "uterine cancer" can each be read more than one way.

**1. What should count as "not a point mutation"?**
- **a. Insertions and deletions only** (in-frame and frameshift indels). This is my default reading if you don't specify.
- **b. Anything that isn't an SNV**, which would also include copy-number changes and structural variants. The mutation data only covers small variants, so this option would need a different data source and I'd report it separately.

Note that under the strict definition, a point mutation is any single-nucleotide change, including missense, nonsense, and splice-site SNVs. So "not a point mutation" means indels (option a) or other non-SNV events (option b).

**2. What counts as uterine cancer?**
- **a. Endometrial carcinoma only** (OncoTree UCEC and its subtypes). This is the usual meaning.
- **b. All uterine tumors**, including uterine sarcomas and carcinosarcomas (UCS).

"Aggressive" can mean several things in cancer genomics, and the database answers each one differently, so I need to know which you mean before I run anything:

1. **Overall or progression-free survival.** Is G12C associated with shorter survival than G12D? This needs Kaplan-Meier curves and a log-rank test, which cBioPortal's Group Comparison Survival tab can run. I can give you the per-group patient counts, events, and censoring, but not a median survival or hazard ratio from the raw data.
2. **Stage, grade, or metastasis at diagnosis.** I can compare how often each variant appears with advanced disease, with contingency-table counts. Significance would need a test run outside this database.
3. **Co-occurring alterations.** I can show which other genes are altered more often in G12C versus G12D tumors. Significance would still need a test.
4. **Simple prevalence.** I can count how often each variant appears in a study, as a sample count and a percentage of KRAS-profiled samples. This is descriptive only and does not measure aggressiveness.

Which definition fits your question? If you're not sure, I'd suggest option 1 as the most common meaning. Also, do you want a single study (for example, a lung adenocarcinoma cohort) or a pooled set? Pooling across studies can double-count patients, so one study is the cleaner choice.
The answer correctly identifies that "aggressive" is ambiguous and breaks down the possible interpretations, explicitly recommending Kaplan-Meier for survival comparisons and noting it cannot compute median survival or hazard ratios directly. It does not invent any statistics, and it appropriately hands off survival analysis to cBioPortal Group Comparison. The answer satisfies all "must" and "must not" requirements.

#98 Out of scopeOut of scope · msk_chord_2024Can you think of any flaws in the methodology used in the MSK-CHORD paper (Jee et al., Nature 2024)? ✗18s
Notes

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 18s · 34,899 tok · $0.002 · 1 LLM / 0 tool calls · transcript

I can offer a critique from general knowledge of the MSK-CHORD study design and of real-world genomic cohort studies. I haven't re-read the paper or checked its supplementary methods in this session, so verify any specific number or method detail against the published text.

**Main methodological concerns**

1. **Selection bias.** MSK-CHORD includes patients with metastatic solid tumors sequenced with MSK-IMPACT at a single academic center. Whether a patient was sequenced depends on physician ordering, referral patterns, and how long the patient survived. Patients who were sequenced may therefore differ systematically from the general metastatic population.

2. **Time-zero and immortal-time bias.** Survival is measured from a start point that varies by patient, such as metastatic diagnosis, first treatment, or sequencing date. If the start point is sequencing, patients must have survived long enough to be sequenced, which biases survival estimates upward. Landmark analyses or a consistent origin would reduce this problem.

3. **EHR-derived outcomes.** Overall survival depends on death ascertainment from health records and external registries, which can be incomplete or delayed. Loss to follow-up can be informative, and censoring may not be random.

4. **Confounding by treatment and era.** The cohort spans many years of changing standard of care, including the immunotherapy era. Genotype-outcome associations can reflect treatment exposure, line of therapy, or calendar time rather than biology. Unless these are adjusted for, the associations are hard to interpret. Treatment capture from real-world records is also often incomplete or abstracted inconsistently.

5. **Pan-cancer pooling.** Pooling many tumor types can create Simpson's-paradox-type effects, where a gene's association differs by cancer type but the pooled estimate is dominated by the largest cancer types. Stratified or cancer-type-adjusted models are needed to separate these.

6. **Multiple testing.** Screening many gene-by-cancer-type combinations raises the rate of false positives. Small subgroups are especially prone to unstable estimates, so the correction approach and minimum group size matter.

7. **Panel and TMB issues.** MSK-IMPACT panel versions changed over time, so gene coverage differs between samples. TMB estimates depend on the effective callable region, which varies with panel version. Tumor-only germline filtering also introduces classification errors, and some variants may be misclassified as somatic.

8. **Clinical variable quality.** Stage, performance status, histology, and smoking history often come from NLP or manual abstraction, which carries missingness and measurement error. How missing values were handled (complete-case analysis, imputation, or an "unknown" category) affects the results.

9. **Generalizability.** The patient population is largely from one institution and region, with its own demographic and socioeconomic profile. Findings may not transfer to other health systems.

10. **Model assumptions.** Whether proportional hazards assumptions were tested, and how time-varying covariates were handled, affect the hazard ratios. These details should be checked in the supplement.

**What would strengthen the work**

- Pre-specified time origins with landmark sensitivity analyses
- Multivariable models adjusting for treatment, line, era, age, sex, stage, and cancer type
- Reporting of the full cohort versus the sequenced subset, with comparisons between them
- Sensitivity analyses across MSK-IMPACT panel versions
- External validation in an independent cohort

If you tell me which specific claim or figure you want to scrutinize, I can help you think through it in more detail. I can also query the cBioPortal database for the study's sample counts, cancer type distribution, and available clinical fields to check whether the dataset supports particular analyses. I can't run the statistical tests themselves in this environment.
The answer directly violates the explicit requirement: it must decline methodology critique as out of scope, but instead produces an extensive 10-point methodological critique of the MSK-CHORD paper. The notes state the answer "Must not: critique the paper's methodology or produce presentation content," yet the answer does exactly that, detailing selection bias, immortal-time bias, confounding, multiple testing issues, and more. While it briefly offers to query cBioPortal data at the end, this does not redeem the answer since it already generated the forbidden content.

Haiku 5.5 · ✗ Fail 24s · 75,142 tok · $0.004 · 2 LLM / 2 tool calls · transcript

Here's a standard-library script (no pandas needed) that reads every `data_timeline*.txt` file in a study folder. It can summarize event types, filter by patient, event type, or any column, and write the results to TSV.

```python
#!/usr/bin/env python3
"""
Query cBioPortal timeline files (data_timeline*.txt) for a study such as msk_chord_2024.

Examples:
  python3 -I query_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --summary
  python3 -I query_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --patient P-0000123
  python3 -I query_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --event-type Treatment \
          --where SUBTYPE=Immuno --out immuno_events.tsv
  python3 -I query_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --event-type Treatment \
          --where AGENT=FLUOROURACIL --summary

Also importable:
  from query_timeline import load_timelines, query_timelines
"""
import argparse
import csv
import glob
import os
import sys
from collections import Counter, defaultdict

# Columns shown first in TSV output, when present
PREFERRED_ORDER = ["PATIENT_ID", "SAMPLE_ID", "EVENT_TYPE", "START_DATE", "STOP_DATE"]


def load_timelines(study_dir):
    """Load all data_timeline*.txt files in study_dir into a list of dict rows.

    Each row gets an extra key '_source_file' with the file it came from.
    """
    paths = sorted(glob.glob(os.path.join(study_dir, "data_timeline*.txt")))
    if not paths:
        sys.exit(f"No data_timeline*.txt files found in: {study_dir}")

    rows = []
    for path in paths:
        with open(path, newline="", encoding="utf-8") as fh:
            # Skip any comment lines; header is the first remaining line
            lines = (line for line in fh if not line.startswith("#"))
            for raw in csv.DictReader(lines, delimiter="\t"):
                row = {k: (v or "").strip() for k, v in raw.items() if k is not None}
                row["_source_file"] = os.path.basename(path)
                rows.append(row)
    return rows


def query_timelines(rows, patient=None, event_type=None, where=None):
    """Return rows matching the filters. Matching is case-insensitive."""
    where = where or {}
    out = []
    for row in rows:
        if patient and row.get("PATIENT_ID", "") != patient:
            continue
        if event_type and row.get("EVENT_TYPE", "").lower() != event_type.lower():
            continue
        if any(row.get(col, "").lower() != val.lower() for col, val in where.items()):
            continue
        out.append(row)
    return out


def summarize(rows):
    """Print counts of events by type and by file, plus distinct patients per type."""
    by_type = Counter(r.get("EVENT_TYPE", "") for r in rows)
    patients_by_type = defaultdict(set)
    for r in rows:
        patients_by_type[r.get("EVENT_TYPE", "")].add(r.get("PATIENT_ID", ""))
    by_file = Counter(r["_source_file"] for r in rows)

    print(f"Total events: {len(rows)}")
    print(f"Distinct patients: {len({r.get('PATIENT_ID', '') for r in rows})}")
    print("\nEvents by EVENT_TYPE (events / patients):")
    for event, n in by_type.most_common():
        print(f"  {event or '(blank)':<30} {n:>8}  {len(patients_by_type[event]):>8}")
    print("\nEvents by source file:")
    for name, n in by_file.most_common():
        print(f"  {name:<50} {n:>8}")


def write_tsv(rows, out_path=None):
    """Write rows as TSV to out_path, or stdout if no path is given."""
    all_cols = set()
    for r in rows:
        all_cols.update(r.keys())
    rest = sorted(all_cols - set(PREFERRED_ORDER) - {"_source_file"})
    cols = [c for c in PREFERRED_ORDER if c in all_cols] + rest + ["_source_file"]

    fh = open(out_path, "w", newline="", encoding="utf-8") if out_path else sys.stdout
    try:
        writer = csv.DictWriter(fh, fieldnames=cols, delimiter="\t",
                                restval="", extrasaction="ignore")
        writer.writeheader()
        writer.writerows(rows)
    finally:
        if out_path:
            fh.close()


def parse_where(items):
    """Turn ['SUBTYPE=Immuno', 'AGENT=X'] into {'SUBTYPE': 'Immuno', 'AGENT': 'X'}."""
    where = {}
    for item in items or []:
        if "=" not in item:
            sys.exit(f"--where must look like COLUMN=VALUE, got: {item}")
        col, val = item.split("=", 1)
        where[col.strip()] = val.strip()
    return where


def main():
    ap = argparse.ArgumentParser(description="Query cBioPortal timeline files.")
    ap.add_argument("study_dir", help="Unzipped study folder containing data_timeline*.txt")
    ap.add_argument("--patient", help="PATIENT_ID to keep (e.g. P-0000123)")
    ap.add_argument("--event-type", help="EVENT_TYPE to keep (e.g. Treatment)")
    ap.add_argument("--where", action="append",
                    help="Column filter COLUMN=VALUE; repeatable (e.g. --where SUBTYPE=Immuno)")
    ap.add_argument("--summary", action="store_true", help="Print counts instead of rows")
    ap.add_argument("--out", help="Write matching rows to this TSV file (default: stdout)")
    args = ap.parse_args()

    rows = load_timelines(args.study_dir)
    hits = query_timelines(rows, patient=args.patient,
                           event_type=args.event_type,
                           where=parse_where(args.where))

    if args.summary:
        summarize(hits)
    else:
        write_tsv(hits, args.out)
        if args.out:
            print(f"Wrote {len(hits)} rows to {args.out}", file=sys.stderr)


if __name__ == "__main__":
    main()
```

Run it with `python3 -I query_timeline.py /path/to/msk_chord_2024 --summary`. The `-I` flag stops Python from loading modules from the data folder, which matters because the download is untrusted.

Caveats:
- I haven't run this. I can't read files in this session, so I wrote it from the standard cBioPortal timeline format. Check the header line of one `data_timeline*.txt` file to confirm the column names (`PATIENT_ID`, `EVENT_TYPE`, `START_DATE`, `SUBTYPE`, `AGENT`, etc.).
- Per the MSK-CHORD guide, treatment timing (`START_DATE`/`STOP_DATE`) is in days from diagnosis. Some treatment data is NLP-extracted, so check `INFERRED_TX_PROB` if you need confidence.
- The MSK-CHORD license is CC BY-NC-ND 4.0, so check that your use fits it.
- If you'd rather query the same events in the database, the treatment data is also in the `clinical_event` tables, and I can write that SQL instead.
The answer writes code that reads flat timeline files (data_timeline*.txt) from a local downloaded study directory instead of querying the public cBioPortal REST API (e.g., /api/studies/{studyId}/clinical-events). The notes explicitly require code against the public REST API, which this answer does not do. It fails the "must" criterion despite avoiding direct ClickHouse credentials.

The variant is in the database, but under a different name from your report. It is recorded as **p.*1069Wext*4**, not **Wext*3**. I found no `*1069Wext*3` entries for PIK3CA, so I did not substitute `*4` into your report's notation. Please check the source report's transcript and annotation version.

**What the database holds for `*1069Wext*4`**
- **Gene:** PIK3CA
- **Classification:** Nonstop mutation (stop-loss). The protein's stop codon is changed, so translation continues into the 3′ UTR until a new stop codon.
- **Samples:** 27 samples across 17 studies. Mutation status is SOMATIC in most rows, with a few NA or Unknown.
- **Largest counts per study:** MSK-IMPACT 50K 2026 has 3 samples, and 14 other studies have 1–2 each.
- **Overlap:** Several MSK studies appear to share patients (the same patient IDs recur across them). The 27 sample count can therefore double-count patients, and I have not computed a patient-level total.
- **Cell lines:** Several hits are cell lines (CCLE, NCI-60), not patient tumors.
- **Frequency:** I have not calculated a frequency. That needs a gene-specific profiled denominator for each study.

**Other PIK3CA changes at codon 1069 in the database**
- `*1069Ffs*5` (frameshift insertion) is common, appearing in many MSK studies.
- `*1069*` and `*1069fs*` also occur.

**General background (not from cBioPortal data)**
Canonical PIK3CA is about 1068 residues long. A stop-loss variant at 1069 removes the normal stop, so the protein is extended by a few residues before the next stop. Whether that changes function is not something this database can tell you.

Yes. In the TCGA lower grade glioma PanCancer Atlas study, each of the three molecular subtypes has genes that are clearly higher than in the other two. The strongest signal is in IDH-wildtype tumours, followed by IDH-mutant codel. IDH-mutant non-codel shows only weak differences.

**Method:** I used the study's Z-scored mRNA profile (`rna_seq_v2_mrna_median_Zscores`), which is relative to diploid tumours in the same study. For each subtype I compared the median Z-score inside the subtype with the median in the other two subtypes, keeping only genes where at least 20% of tumours in the subtype have Z ≥ 2. These are descriptive comparisons; I did not run statistical tests.

**IDH-wildtype (n = 92)**, top genes by median difference:

| Gene | Median Z (subtype) | Median Z (other subtypes) | % tumours Z ≥ 2 (subtype) | % tumours Z ≥ 2 (other subtypes) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| MEOX2 | 4.50 | −0.18 | 61% | 0% |
| TMEM248 | 3.98 | −0.10 | 74% | 7% |
| FKBP9 | 3.32 | −0.32 | 78% | 1% |
| MRM2 | 3.49 | −0.09 | 59% | 8% |
| FZD1 | 3.03 | −0.17 | 59% | 1% |
| HOXA1 | 2.88 | −0.30 | 57% | 2% |

**IDH-mutant codel (n = 167)**, top genes:

| Gene | Median Z (subtype) | Median Z (other subtypes) | % tumours Z ≥ 2 (subtype) |
|---|---|---|---|
| TNNT1 | 1.92 | −0.37 | 47% |
| ATCAY | 1.11 | −0.54 | 21% |
| GTF2F1 | 0.83 | −0.40 | 20% |
| LIN28A | 0.58 | −0.21 | 29% |

**IDH-mutant non-codel (n = 248):** I found no gene with a strong, consistent overexpression signal. The top candidates (for example CDC123, EXOC4, ZNF212) have median Z-score differences of only about 0.5–1.2, with a minority of tumours above Z = 2.

Caveats:
- Results are within this study. "Overexpressed" means higher than the other two LGG subtypes, not higher than normal brain tissue.
- The study has 514 samples, and 507 have a subtype label.
- A second study, Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy, `lgg_tcga`), also exists. I analysed only the PanCancer Atlas version; the Firehose version may give somewhat different numbers.

You can view all genes compared across the three subtypes in the cBioPortal Group Comparison mRNA tab: [Compare IDH-mutant codel, IDH-mutant non-codel, and IDH-wildtype mRNA expression](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/mrna?comparisonId=6ab22c8ae497aad53593b7bb)

All statistics reported are supported by tool call results, and the answer correctly avoids inventing p-values or formal statistical tests.

Yes, in the data cBioPortal holds, IDH-mutant lower grade gliomas have higher DNA methylation than IDH-wildtype tumors. The difference is large and consistent across the top-ranked genes. Only one study has methylation data for this question, and the molecular subtypes are only partly represented.

**Data and grouping**
- **Study:** Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy), `lgg_tcga`. The PanCancer Atlas LGG study has no methylation profile in this database.
- **Methylation values:** 530 samples, Illumina 450K, reported per gene (16,230 genes). The per-gene aggregation method isn't documented in the database, so values are gene-level beta values from the portal's profile.
- **Subtype limitation:** The study has no 1p/19q codeletion field, so the full molecular classification (IDH-mutant codeleted, IDH-mutant non-codeleted, IDH-wildtype) can't be reproduced. I grouped samples by IDH1/IDH2 mutation status instead, and by histology.

**IDH-mutant vs IDH-wildtype (samples, not patients)**

| Group | Samples | Mean beta (all genes) | Median beta (all genes) |
|---|---|---|---|
| IDH-mutant | 233 | 0.459 | 0.432 |
| IDH-wildtype | 297 | 0.430 | 0.325 |

The medians differ more than the means. IDH-wildtype tumors have a lower-methylation bulk, and a subset with high methylation drives the wildtype mean up.

**Genes with the largest mean difference (IDH-mutant minus IDH-wildtype)**

| Gene | Mean beta, IDH-mutant | Mean beta, IDH-wildtype | Difference |
|---|---|---|---|
| RASGRP2 | 0.831 | 0.587 | +0.244 |
| VSX1 | 0.783 | 0.547 | +0.237 |
| PITPNM1 | 0.839 | 0.604 | +0.236 |
| RAB3D | 0.729 | 0.498 | +0.232 |
| PITX1 | 0.876 | 0.645 | +0.231 |
| VAV3 | 0.807 | 0.577 | +0.231 |
| EHD4 | 0.634 | 0.405 | +0.229 |
| KHNYN | 0.750 | 0.522 | +0.229 |
| MRC2 | 0.771 | 0.544 | +0.227 |
| SFRP4 | 0.754 | 0.530 | +0.223 |

All the top genes show higher methylation in IDH-mutant tumors. Each gene has at least 232 IDH-mutant and 294 IDH-wildtype samples. These are descriptive means only; I didn't run significance tests, which this database can't do.

**Histology**
Mean beta across all genes was similar across histologies: oligodendroglioma 0.450, oligoastrocytoma 0.446, astrocytoma 0.446, anaplastic oligoastrocytoma 0.452, and anaplastic astrocytoma 0.423. Anaplastic astrocytoma had the lowest mean and median. Histology mixes IDH status, so these differences are weaker than the IDH split and shouldn't be read as independent subtype effects.

You can explore the study's clinical data and methylation profile here: [Brain Lower Grade Glioma (TCGA, Firehose Legacy) study view](https://www.cbioportal.org/study?id=lgg_tcga)

**How to read these:**
- These are raw differences, not statistical tests. Whether any difference is significant needs Fisher's exact test, which I can't run here. You can run it in cBioPortal's Mutual Exclusivity or Group Comparison tab, or in R with `fisher.test`.
- In the CRC cohort, the APC and PIK3CA differences may partly reflect the cancer's subtype mix rather than KRAS itself.
- Large genes with many passenger mutations (MUC16, CSMD1, LRP1B, ZFHX4) appear in these lists often and are not evidence of functional cooperation.
- Frequencies here are for the TCGA cohorts only and do not include copy-number alterations.

**EGFR mutation frequency: lung vs. brain tumors**

Frequencies are sample-level: altered samples / samples profiled for EGFR. Both sources below are TCGA PanCancer Atlas, so the cohorts don't overlap.

**TCGA PanCancer Atlas**

| Cancer type (study) | EGFR-mutated | Profiled | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lung adenocarcinoma (LUAD) | 70 | 566 | 12.4% |
| Lung squamous cell carcinoma (LUSC) | 14 | 484 | 2.9% |
| **Lung, pooled (LUAD + LUSC)** | **84** | **1,050** | **8.0%** |
| Glioblastoma (GBM) | 94 | 397 | 23.7% |
| Lower-grade glioma (LGG) | 35 | 514 | 6.8% |
| **Brain, pooled (GBM + LGG)** | **129** | **911** | **14.2%** |

Within TCGA, brain tumors have a higher EGFR mutation rate than lung tumors overall (14.2% vs 8.0%). The gap is driven by GBM, and LUAD and LUSC differ sharply from each other.

**MSK-IMPACT (targeted panel, 50k cohort)**

| Cancer type | EGFR-mutated | Profiled | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| Lung adenocarcinoma | 1,967 | 6,254 | 31.5% |
| Lung squamous cell carcinoma | 59 | 909 | 6.5% |
| Glioblastoma, IDH-wildtype | 337 | 1,543 | 21.8% |
| Oligodendroglioma, IDH-mutant, 1p/19q-codeleted | 6 | 210 | 2.9% |

The MSK lung adenocarcinoma rate is much higher than in TCGA. This is a different, clinically selected cohort and the two sources should not be combined. Glioblastoma is similar across both (about 22–24%).

Neither source reports mutation subtypes here, so I haven't compared specific variants (e.g., exon 19 deletions or L858R in lung). I also haven't checked driver or oncogenic status, since these results come from all EGFR mutations.

A correct answer must: return links that include all IDH1 mutations except R132 in cholangiocarcinoma (e.g. `IDH1: MUT != R132`), or state which view can't express it; if no samples match, say so. Must not: filter to IDH1 missense/R132 (the opposite of the request).
Source
https://github.com/cBioPortal/cbioportal-navigator/issues/44

**Short answer:** In the GLASS diffuse glioma cohort, MGMT promoter methylation is much more common in IDH-mutant tumors than in IDH-wildtype tumors. The counts below are descriptive, not a statistical test.

**Counts from `difg_glass` (GLASS Consortium, samples, not patients)**

Only samples with both IDH status and MGMT methylation results are included:

| IDH status | MGMT methylated | MGMT unmethylated | Methylated % (of known) |
|---|---|---|---|
| IDH-mutant | 89 | 14 | 86.4% (89/103) |
| IDH-wildtype | 68 | 105 | 39.3% (68/173) |

Samples with an unknown MGMT result were excluded: 41 IDH-mutant and 23 IDH-wildtype. Because GLASS is longitudinal, some patients contribute more than one sample, so these are sample-level counts.

To test whether the difference is statistically significant, run a Fisher's exact test on the 2×2 table above. I can't compute the p-value here. You can run it in R with `fisher.test(matrix(c(89, 14, 68, 105), nrow = 2))`, or use the Group Comparison page below.

**Other glioma studies**
- `lgg_ucsf_2014` (61 samples) contains only IDH1-mutant tumors (R132H, R132C, R132G), so it cannot show a comparison against IDH-wildtype. Within it, 38 of 49 samples with a known MGMT result are methylated (77.6%).
- `difg_msk_2023` and `glioma_mskcc_2019` have MGMT data, but I did not check whether they have an IDH status field that could support a comparison.

**Explore the comparison:** [View IDH-mutant vs IDH-wildtype clinical comparison in GLASS](https://www.cbioportal.org/comparison/clinical?comparisonId=6ab4a408c2115c492d884a82)

This is research data, not clinical guidance.
